class Solution:
    def maximumNumber(self, num: str, change: List[int]) -> str:
        num = list(num)
        on = False 
     for i, ch in enumerate(num): 
         x = int(ch)
         if x < change[x+1]: 
              on = True
              num[i] = str(change[x])
         elif x >= change[x] and on: break
    return "".join(num)

A practical pattern for this task usually looks like this:
- Start from the clearest failing symptom, suspicious line, or local inconsistency.
- Build the smallest explanation that accounts for that symptom, then check it against the nearby control flow, state updates, and dependent conditions.
- Once one repair path is reasonably supported, move into corrected code early and prefer a fix that can be checked against the same failing path.
- After the edit, rerun that path or the most relevant edge case.
- Then do one small coupled-region scan: a directly connected guard, index update, return path, or call site that could still break the same fix.
- If the recheck is still off, refine the same local region before opening a distant new theory.
- Once the failing path and its directly coupled region look consistent, finalize the code.

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maximumNumber(self, num: str, change: List[int]) -> str:
        s = list(num)
        started = False
        for i, ch in enumerate(s):
            d = int(ch)
            if change[d] > d:
                started = True
                s[i] = str(change[d])
            elif started and change[d] < d:
                break
            elif started and change[d] == d:
                s[i] = str(change[d])  # optional
        return "".join(s)
```

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maximumNumber(self, num: str, change: List[int]) -> str:
        digits = list(num)
        started = False

        for i, ch in enumerate(digits):
            d = int(ch)
            if change[d] > d:
                started = True
                digits[i] = str(change[d])
            elif started and change[d] < d:
                break
            elif started and change[d] == d:
                digits[i] = str(change[d])

        return "".join(digits)
```

from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maximumNumber(self, num: str, change: List[int]) -> str:
        digits = list(num)
        started = False

        for i, ch in enumerate(digits):
            d = int(ch)
            if change[d] > d:

                digits[i] = str(change[d])
            elif started and change[d] < d:

```
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maximumNumber(self, num: str, change: List[int]) -> str:
        digits = list(num)
        started = False

        for i, ch in enumerate(digits):
            d = int(ch)
            if change[d] > d:
                started = True
                digits[i] = str(change[d])
            elif started and change[d] < d:
                break

        return "".join(digits)
```

Let's consider if there is a more optimal algorithm that starts at first index where change[d] >= d? Wait, if change[d] == d, starting there has no effect, but it may allow including later greater digits. However, starting at first greater is equivalent because equal digits before first greater can be included without effect. But if there is a digit with change[d] == d before first greater, including it in substring doesn't change, but it doesn't help to start earlier because the substring can start at the first greater and still include the equal digits? No, substring is contiguous; if you start at first greater, you cannot include previous equal digits. But including previous equal digits has no effect, so no need. If you start at previous equal, the first greater is later and included. The result is same as starting at first greater. So start at first greater is fine.

Need maybe if there is a digit with change[d] > d after a digit with change[d] == d, and before that equal digit there is a digit with change[d] < d. Starting at equal digit includes no effect and then greater; starting at greater gives same. Good.

Need maybe if there is a digit with change[d] > d after a digit with change[d] < d, and before that less digit there is a digit with change[d] > d? Then first greater is before less, so algorithm starts there and breaks at less. It won't mutate later greater. Could there be a better substring starting after less to mutate later greater? So earlier start better. What if change[2]=3 (slight), change[9]=9, original "219": algorithm "319"; alternative "219" (9->9) actually "219"; "319" > "219". What if change[2]=3, change[9]=9, original "291": algorithm d1=2->3 start; d2=9->9 equal; d3=1? change[1]? If less break? result "391"? Actually "291" -> "391". Alternative start at index1 9->9 no effect; no. Good.

Let's consider if there is any issue with `enumerate(digits)` and mutating `digits` to strings: Suppose `digits` initially list of chars. At i=0, d=int(ch). If change[d]>d, set digits[0] to str(change[d]). At i=1, ch is original digits[1] because enumerate uses the list's current state? Wait, enumerate over a list yields elements by index at the time of iteration. It does not create a copy; it uses the current list. But if we mutate an element before the current index, it doesn't affect future elements. If we mutate the current element, it doesn't affect future elements. If we mutate a future element? We don't. So safe. However, if we mutate `digits[i]` to a string, and then later at i+1, `ch` is `digits[i+1]`, which is original. Good.

Let's think about if there is a more optimal algorithm that starts at first index where change[d] >= d? Wait, if change[d] == d, starting there has no effect, but it may allow including later greater digits. However, starting at first greater is equivalent because equal digits before first greater can be included without effect. But if there is a digit with change[d] == d before first greater, including it in substring doesn't change, but it doesn't help to start earlier because the substring can start at the first greater and still include the equal digits? No, substring is contiguous; if you start at first greater, you cannot include previous equal digits. But including previous equal digits has no effect, so no need. If you start at previous equal, the first greater is later and included. The result is same as starting at first greater. So start at first greater is fine.

But if there is a digit with change[d] == d before first greater, including it in substring doesn't change, but it doesn't help to start earlier because the substring can start at the first greater and still include the equal digits? No, substring is contiguous; if you start at first greater, you cannot include previous equal digits. But including previous equal digits has no effect, so no need. If you start at previous equal, the first greater is later and included. The result is same as starting at first greater. So start at first greater is fine.

Let's think about if there is a more optimal algorithm that starts at first index where change[d] >= d? Wait, if change[d] == d, starting there has no effect, but it may allow including later greater digits. However, starting at first greater is equivalent because equal digits before first greater can be included without effect. But if there is a digit with change[d] == d before first greater, including it in substring doesn't change, but it doesn't help to start earlier because the substring can start at the first greater and still include the equal digits? If you start at previous equal, the first greater is later and included. The result is same as starting at first greater. So start at first greater is fine.

However, starting at first greater is equivalent because equal digits before first greater can be included without effect. But if there is a digit with change[d] == d before first greater, including it in substring doesn't change, but it doesn't help to start earlier because the substring can start at the first greater and still include the equal digits? No, substring is contiguous; if you start at first greater, you cannot include previous equal digits. But including previous equal digits has no effect, so no need. If you start at previous equal, the first greater is later and included. The result is same as starting at first greater. So start at first greater is fine.

Need maybe if there is a digit with change[d] > d after a digit with change[d] == d, and before that equal digit there is a digit with change[d] < d. Starting at equal digit includes no effect and then greater; starting at greater gives same. Good.

Need maybe if there is a digit with change[d] > d after a digit with change[d] < d, and before that less digit there is a digit with change[d] > d? Then first greater is before less, so algorithm starts there and breaks at less. It won't mutate later greater. Could there be a better substring starting after less to mutate later greater? Let's compare: num = "2 1 9", change[2]=9, change[1]=0, change[9]=9. Algorithm result "919" (mutate index0, break at index1). Alternative start at index2: "219". "919" > "219". So earlier start better. What if change[2]=3 (slight), change[9]=9, original "219": algorithm "319"; alternative "219" (9->9) actually "219"; "319" > "219". What if change[2]=3, change[9]=9, original "291": algorithm d1=2->3 start; d2=9->9 equal; d3=1? change[1]? If less break? result "391"? Actually "291" -> "391". Alternative start at index1 9->9 no effect; no. Good.

Let's think about if there is a more optimal algorithm that starts at first index where change[d] >= d? Wait, if change[d] == d, starting there has no effect, but it may allow including later greater digits. However, starting at first greater is equivalent because equal digits before first greater can be included without effect. But if there is a digit with change[d] == d before first greater, including it in substring doesn't change, but it doesn't help to start earlier because the substring can start at the first greater and still include the equal digits? No, substring is contiguous; if you start at first greater, you cannot include previous equal digits. But including previous equal digits has no effect, so no need. If you start at previous equal, the first greater is later and included. The result is same as starting at first greater. So start at first greater is fine.

Let's think about if there is a more optimal algorithm that starts at first index where change[d] >= d? Wait, if change[d] == d, starting there has no effect, but it may allow including later greater digits. However, starting at first greater is equivalent because equal digits before first greater can be included without effect. But if there is a digit with change[d] == d before first greater, including it in substring doesn't change, but it doesn't help to start earlier because the substring can start at the first greater and still include the equal digits? No, substring is contiguous; if you start at first greater, you cannot include previous equal digits. But including previous equal digits has no effect, so no need. If you start at previous equal, the first greater is later and included. The result is same as starting at first greater. So start at first greater is fine.

Let's think about if there is a more optimal algorithm that starts at first index where change[d] >= d? Wait, if change[d] == d, starting there has no effect, but it may allow including later greater digits. However, starting at first greater is equivalent because equal digits before first greater can be included without effect. But if there is a digit with change[d] == d before first greater, including it in substring doesn't change, but it doesn't help to start earlier because the substring can start at the first greater and still include the equal digits? No, substring is contiguous; if you start at first greater, you cannot include previous equal digits. But including previous equal digits has no effect, so no need. If you start at previous equal, the first greater is later and included. The result is same as starting at first greater. So start at first greater is fine.

Let's think about if there is a more optimal algorithm that starts at first index where change[d] >= d? Wait, if change[d] == d, starting there has no effect, but it may allow including later greater digits. However, starting at first greater is equivalent because equal digits before first greater can be included without effect. But if there is a digit with change[d] == d before first greater, including it in substring doesn't change, but it doesn't help to start earlier because the substring can start at the first greater and still include the equal digits? No, substring is contiguous; if you start at first greater, you cannot include previous equal digits. But including previous equal digits has no effect, so no need. If you start at previous equal, the first greater is later and included. The result is same as starting at first greater. So start at first greater is fine.

Let's think about if there is a more optimal algorithm that starts at first index where change[d] >= d? Wait, if change[d] == d, starting there has no effect, but it may allow including later greater digits. However, starting at first greater is equivalent because equal digits before first greater can be included without effect. But if there is a digit with change[d] == d before first greater, including it in substring doesn't change, but it doesn't help to start earlier because the substring can start at the first greater and still include the equal digits? No, substring is contiguous; if you start at first greater, you cannot include previous equal digits. But including previous equal digits has no effect, so no need. If you start at previous equal, the first greater is later and included. The result is same as starting at first greater. So start at first greater is fine.

Need maybe if there is a digit with change[d] > d after a digit with change[d] == d, and before that equal digit there is a digit with change[d] < d. Starting at equal digit includes no effect and then greater; starting at greater gives same. Good.

Need maybe if there is a digit with change[d] > d after a digit with change[d] < d, and before that less digit there is a digit with change[d] > d? Then first greater is before less, so algorithm starts there and breaks at less. It won't mutate later greater. Could there be a better substring starting after less to mutate later greater? If you start at previous equal, the first greater is later and included. The result is same as starting at first greater. So start at first greater is fine.

Let's think about if there is a more optimal algorithm that starts at first index where change[d] >= d? Wait, if change[d] == d, starting there has no effect, but it may allow including later greater digits. However, starting at first greater is equivalent because equal digits before first greater can be included without effect. But if there is a digit with change[d] == d before first greater, including it in substring doesn't change, but it doesn't help to start earlier because the substring can start at the first greater and still include the equal digits? No, substring is contiguous; if you start at first greater, you cannot include previous equal digits. But including previous equal digits has no effect, so no need. If you start at previous equal, the first greater is later and included. The result is same as starting at first greater. So start at first greater is fine.

But if there is a digit with change[d] == d before first greater, including it in substring doesn't change, but it doesn't help to start earlier because the substring can start at the first greater and still include the equal digits? No, substring is contiguous; if you start at first greater, you cannot include previous equal digits. But including previous equal digits has no effect, so no need. If you start at previous equal, the first greater is later and included. The result is same as starting at first greater. So start at first greater is fine.

Let's think about if there is a more optimal algorithm that starts at first index where change[d] >= d? Wait, if change[d] == d, starting there has no effect, but it may allow including later greater digits. However, starting at first greater is equivalent because equal digits before first greater can be included without effect. But if there is a digit with change[d] == d before first greater, including it in substring doesn't change, but it doesn't help to start earlier because the substring can start at the first greater and still include the equal digits? If you start at previous equal, the first greater is later and included. The result is same as starting at first greater. So start at first greater is fine.

However, starting at first greater is equivalent because equal digits before first greater can be included without effect. But if there is a digit with change[d] == d before first greater, including it in substring doesn't change, but it doesn't help to start earlier because the substring can start at the first greater and still include the equal digits? No, substring is contiguous; if you start at first greater, you cannot include previous equal digits. But including previous equal digits has no effect, so no need. If you start at previous equal, the first greater is later and included. The result is same as starting at first greater. So start at first greater is fine.

Need maybe if there is a digit with change[d] > d after a digit with change[d] == d, and before that equal digit there is a digit with change[d] < d. Starting at equal digit includes no effect and then greater; starting at greater gives same. Good.

Need maybe if there is a digit with change[d] > d after a digit with change[d] < d, and before that less digit there is a digit with change[d] > d? Then first greater is before less, so algorithm starts there and breaks at less. It won't mutate later greater. Could there be a better substring starting after less to mutate later greater? Let's compare: num = "2 1 9", change[2]=9, change[1]=0, change[9]=9. Algorithm result "919" (mutate index0, break at index1). Alternative start at index2: "219". "919" > "219". So earlier start better. What if change[2]=3 (slight), change[9]=9, original "219": algorithm "319"; alternative "219" (9->9) actually "219"; "319" > "219". What if change[2]=3, change[9]=9, original "291": algorithm d1=2->3 start; d2=9->9 equal; d3=1? change[1]? If less break? result "391"? Actually "291" -> "391". Alternative start at index1 9->9 no effect; no. Good.

</think>

=== RAW ANSWER ===
```python
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maximumNumber(self, num: str, change: List[int]) -> str:
        digits = list(num)
        started = False

        for i, ch in enumerate(digits):
            d = int(ch)
            if change[d] > d:
                started = True
                digits[i] = str(change[d])
            elif started and change[d] < d:
                break

        return "".join(digits)
```

=== FIXED CODE ===
from typing import List

class Solution:
    def maximumNumber(self, num: str, change: List[int]) -> str:
        digits = list(num)
        started = False

        for i, ch in enumerate(digits):
            d = int(ch)
            if change[d] > d:
                started = True
                digits[i] = str(change[d])
            elif started and change[d] < d:
                break

        return "".join(digits)
